http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m27-008.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:43]
Re: Starchild And What Annoys Me About The Sceptics

  In short I am both encouraged and disappointed. Encouraged
  because he replied at all and had conceded to watch a TV
  documentary he'd been sent on Starchild.
  I am disappointed because I don't consider he's given the
  research a fair whack - and now says he's not interested in
  pursuing the matter further, which must mean he won't examine
  the skull.
  After my initial enquiry the Professor wrote :
  "Dave, I have had a few enquiries about this previously (e,g,
  see edited emails below) and saw a recent TV documentary about
  it, which included DNA testing, with unremarkable results. Sorry
  to say that I have no interest in pursuing this topic, Best,
  Chris"
  He included a couple of edited emails - one an enquiry like mine
  which presumably included the video of the documentary and
  concluded; "My question is this - after watching the video do
  you consider the skull to be an obvious/probable deformity or of
  more scientific significance? I look forward to hearing from
  you!"
  Professor Stringer included his email reply to this enquirer
  which said: "Presumably you have looked at the Wikipedia entry
  for this - it's quite helpful? DNA work has apparently already
  been done, with unsurprising results, and more would not cost
  anything like as much as =A3250,000. As for this statement 'The
  results uncovered by Pye's team ruled out all known deformities'
  I'm afraid I'm with the sceptics on this one - there are various
  pathologies that will deform a skull like this. I have answered
  some previous enquiries on the skull, but don't have my emails
  at home - however, I did pursue the quoted work at Royal
  Holloway (where I am a Visiting Professor) of unusual bone
  structure, and was unable to recover any useful or
  scientifically verifiable information about this work, or to
  contact the person concerned, who had left several years
  earlier..Best Chris"
  I wrote back to the Professor addressing several issues,
  including why he considered the DNA results 'unremarkable', and
  why he let drop the Royal Holloway enquiry about the unusual
  bone structure, simply because he was unable to speak to the
  person who did the research.
  With his final reply to me he sent impressive photos of deformed
  skulls - one looking very similar to Starchild - and these
  words: "The mtDNA showed this was a normal native American human
  - or at least the mother was, which seems a good starting point
  to me. And I don't see anything really special in the
  pathological skull shape - have a look at these examples below.
  And the man at the Royal Holloway had left and none of the
  results were filed there, or published in a peer review journal,
  as far as I could ascertain. I have no wish to be involved with
  this, thank you, Best Chris"
  So there we have it Dave. I was wrong in saying that an academic
  of his standing wouldn't even look at the Starchild issue. But I
  think I might have been right in that he didn't perhaps pursue
  it with the vigour one might like, to nail whether or not there
  is any real substance to Pye's claims.
  He seems to have agreed for instance that the bone structure is
  unusual but because he couldn't speak to the person who studied
  it - decided to drop the matter.
  Which I guess leaves it back in Lloyd Pye's lap to prove his
  case but to make sure that the scientists he uses are accepted
  experts and the issue is written up in peer reviewed journals.
  Personally I am not holding my breath... but wish him well.

  Dave Haith

http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m27-009.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:43]
Re: Starchild And What Annoys Me About The Sceptics



  An email from Pye to myself might cover this Wikipedia point,
  Gerald.
  (Incidentally I have Pye's permission to post any of his
  correspondence with me on this List - although he doesn't know
  the name of the List. And I have sent nothing to him from the
  List that would contravene the rules on outside posting)
  Pye writes concerning researchers' use of Wikipedia:
  -----
  "as one of their major sources of information, obviously not
  realizing that Wiki is engaged in an all-out and quite open
  "war" against anyone and anything that the graduate students who
  run Wiki think is a challenge in any way to the scientific
  status quo. Thus, their reports about anything in the field of
  Alternative Knowledge are inevitably riddled with falsehoods,
  gross distortions, and outright lies in order to us look as bad
  as possible.

  I have tried for years to get the Starchild's report changed on
  Wiki, just as I've tried to get my own changed. Any change made
  is reversed within a few hours. Both are no doubt linked to some
  grad student's computer so he or she will know about it and the
  reversal back to the original wording can be made. Wiki is
  thoroughly corrupt and useless when it comes to alternative
  ideas and information, but there is simply nothing we can do
  about it. The have all the power and they choose to misuse it
  flagrantly.
  The DNA test they referred to was done in late 1999 and did
  produce a "Starchild is entirely human" result. But it was a
  forensic lab, not a lab equipped to recover and test ancient
  DNA, which the Starchild is. So they made a mistake, plain and
  simple, but that mistake is what Wiki and all other skeptics
  choose to trumpet as THE answer about its DNA. In 2003 another
  DNA test was run at a lab equipped for proper recovery of
  ancient DNA and that result was unequivocal: human mother,
  nonhuman father. But with the primer technology of that time we
  could not determine HOW FAR the father, and therefore the
  Starchild itself, was from normal human.
  That is what the new genome recovery technique of 454 Life
  Sciences will permit us to do, which is recover ALL of the
  Starchild's base pairs to line them up against humans and
  determine EXACTLY HOW FAR from normal it actually was. This is
  what will make history because, despite what Chris Stringer
  says, there are really NO physiological comparisons to be made
  between the Starchild and normal humans. No human deformity
  changes the actual biochemical composition of the bone. I would
  very much doubt that Chris knows the Starchild's bone is more
  like tooth enamel than bone. I doubt Clarke does, either.
  Neither man will bother himself to read as much as the eBook,
  which can be done in an hour, because he's already convinced
  it's a hoax.
  The thing I'm up against, Dave, is a gigantic collection of
  enormous egos who think they ALREADY KNOW all there is to
  possibly know about this subject. They become experts in one
  area of science and they think it makes them experts about
  everything. But when it comes to certain things, like the
  Starchild, the average fan knows more about it than they do
  because everything about it, such as Chris watching a
  documentary clip, comes through their preset filter of: "This
  can't possibly be true, there MUST be a way to explain it away."
  And so in their minds they come up with some kind of silly
  dismissive answer and that becomes IT for them, and nobody is
  going to move them off of it.
  The bottom line is that mainstream scientists like Clarke and
  Stringer are simply NOT going to entertain the idea that they
  might be wrong about something they've already proclaimed to be
  a fraud or a hoax or whatever. In their minds their reputations
  have been placed on the line, and now they are in a position to
  have to DEFEND a prior judgement, even though that judgement was
  made hastily and without even a small portion of the actual
  facts in the case.


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m27-009.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:43]

  I'm utterly exhausted with dealing with people like this, and
  I'm only doing this much because you seem like a nice and
  sincere guy. But I'm telling you, after ten years of experience
  here is how it works: you will NOT change closed minds, or
  arrogant personalities, or fragile egos. It just won't happen.
  The only way to win this argument is to just ignore the
  naysayers, find a way to raise the money to get the test done,
  and then just cram the results down the naysayer throats until
  they gag. It's the only way....truly.
  Lloyd

  Mr. Stanford, when I was investigating the Barney Barnett UFO
  Incident, I had interviewed many witnesses in the Plains of St.
  Agustin and Socorro area. Since there were two famous cases
  overlapping in the same general area, I naturally made record of
  both.
  I happened to interviewed a BLM worker who told me that he heard
  the call made by Zamora on the radio and got to the area soon
  afterwards. He said he smelled something like kerosene burning!
  Have you heard any similar comments from others? Could one of
  the desert plants produce a similar odor when burning and have
  you or others checked out that possibility?
  About twelve years ago a claim was made that the sighting was
  the inadvertent landing and take-off of a hot air balloon which
  seems to fit the witness account above, who'd smelled a strong
  odor of burning kerosene.
  Having been around such balloons, they are extremely loud and
  considerably frightening if you encounter one for the first time
  dropping down from the sky. Their burners produce heat that can


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m27-011.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:44]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  be felt for a considerable distance ( 60 feet and more)! In any
  event, how fast was the wind blowing that day and in which
  direction? And, in which direction did Zamora say that the
  object departed? Is it possible that these two directions
  agreed? In addition, would that place him downwind of the object
  too?
  Has Zamora ever been re-interviewed concerning this possibility?
  Since such Balloons are probably more common these days, has he,
  himself, ever seen one since? If not, it might be an interesting
  proposal to expose him to one up close to get his impressions.
  When the hot air balloon hypothesis was being considered, I
  naturally took more interest in it since it was not too long
  beforehand that I had interviewed this witness - that, along
  with my own personal experience with such balloons had naturally
  peeked my interest.
  Perhaps you can address these issues in your upcoming report. If
  such pranksters can produce evidence of a prank as sophisticated
  as a hot air balloon, there may be some room for discussion(?).
  But, it's very doubtful. Otherwise, I would have to agree with
  you that their claims are rather flimsy, mere hearsay, and do
  not fit the facts, so far, obtained.
  Some examples of hot air balloons:
  The hot air balloon burner generates a level of noise between
  that of a freight train (88 decibels) and a circular saw (107
  decibels).
  The threshold for human noise-induced pain is about 130dB.
  Please watch them all in order to formulate an impression:
  http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3Dbp6UPOgouKw&feature=3Drelated

                                                   It's All Errol And Alfred's Fault
   From: Greg Boone <Evolbaby.nul>
   Date: Sun, 27 Sep 2009 14:45:32 EDT
   Archived: Sun, 27 Sep 2009 17:07:27 -0400
   Subject: It's All Errol And Alfred's Fault

   It's all Errol and Alfred's fault.
   I've been working my butt off the past week and especially in
   strategizing with the animation group I work with. Many of you
   don't know about because I keep it off Errol's list but it's
   called animationnation.com. It's run by all the radicals of the
   animation industry here in Hollywood and we have a stellar cast
   of the world's most famous animators, comics, sci-fi, voice
   actors, talent there. I often bring up the subject of UFOs on
   our message board there and those posts get quite a viral
   exchange.
   This past week we've ramped up things to do more live video
   tutorials for schools globally and I was sitting here trying to
   figure out the programming to embed video and other such website
   goodies. I often point visitors to the UFO Updates List and you
   can bet Hollywood's top studios do tune in to the Updates List.
   We know because we check our stats and emails.
   Well today I'm sitting here trying to be serious and program and
   I end up reading an Alfred Lehmberg post. It was so funny I
   burst out in laughter so hard I almost destroyed my workstation.
   I went into animator's shock and immediately envisioned Alfred
   at his desk suddenly turned into a classic Warner Bros. cartoon
   with a Mel Blanc voice over. I could not work for an hour. I'm
   behind schedule and can't draw because I keep laughing.
   It's all Errol and Alfred's fault. Errol knew that Alfred's post
   was too funny for human minds yet he let it go anyway.
   I showed the post to some pals at Disney and Dreamworks and they
   laughed so hard their cell phones went out. I got blamed for all
   this mind you.
   Alfred you have single handedly floored animators from The
   Simpsons, Family Guy, numerous artists from Marvel Comics,
   Disney, Dreamworks and Sony.
   I may have enough strength left to make it to today's church
   meeting but I know I'm going to get into trouble because I'll
   end up bursting out in laughter and there are dozens of english
   literature experts there and they'll ask me why and I'll have to
   show them Alfred's post and his legendary linguistic legerdemain
   and they're going to end up rolling on the floor and disrupt the
   preachin' and we'll all get in trouble.

   Best,
   Greg

Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast




                                                          UFO Updates
                                   A mailing list for the study of UFO-related phenomena
                                               'Its All Here In Black & White'
    Location: UFOUpDatesList.Com > 2009 > Sep > Sep 28

                                     Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  From: David Rudiak <drudiak.nul>
  Date: Sun, 27 Sep 2009 17:15:50 -0700
  Archived: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 06:08:35 -0400
  Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  >From: Viktor Golubik <Diverge247.nul>
  >To: post.nul
  >Date: Sun, 27 Sep 2009 13:20:36 EDT
  >Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  >>From: Ray Stanford <opus22.nul>
  >>To: UFO UpDates - Toronto <post.nul>
  >>Date: Sun, 27 Sep 2009 01:48:45 -0400
  >>Subject: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  >>I have sent the following to The UFO Iconoclast, in response to
  >>Anthony Bragalia's ludicrous claim that the Socorro CE III case
  >>was engineered by anonymous college students. I doubt it will
  >>get posted, or at least it hasn't as yet.:
  >About twelve years ago a claim was made that the sighting was
  >the inadvertent landing and take-off of a hot air balloon which
  >seems to fit the witness account above, who'd smelled a strong
  >odor of burning kerosene.
  >Having been around such balloons, they are extremely loud and
  >considerably frightening if you encounter one for the first time
  >dropping down from the sky. Their burners produce heat that can
  >be felt for a considerable distance ( 60 feet and more)! In any
  >event, how fast was the wind blowing that day and in which
  >direction?
  >And, in which direction did Zamora say that the
  >object departed? Is it possible that these two directions
  >agreed? In addition, would that place him downwind of the
  >object too?
  Several different balloon theories have been proposed for
  Socorro. All turn out to be preposterous if not impossible when
  the physics and details of the case are analyzed, as they have
  been here on Updates in years past.
  Balloons are passive devices. They are carried in the direction
  of and no faster than the prevailing wind. The wind was
  determined to be out of the south and it was a very breezy day.
  As I remember, wind gusts were up 25 or 30 mph.
  The Socorro object departed to the west-southwest, that is
  bucking the wind. This is quite impossible for a balloon.
  The Socorro object also departed at a speed far greater than the
  prevailing wind. In fact, it may very well have been supersonic.
  The calculations are very simple, Physics 1 stuff. Zamora said
  the object faded in the distance at about 6 miles (using known
  landmarks). He estimated the time from when it went silent, flew
  horizontally towards the mountains in the west, then went into a
  steep climb before fading was about 10 seconds. (Incidentally,
  he had retrieved his glasses and had them on while watching the
  object's departure and disappearance in the distance.) Ray
  Stanford thought 20 seconds was probably more accurate when he
  reconstructed the timeline after interviewing Zamora.
  Let's start with these numbers. 6 miles in 20 seconds is 18
  miles a minute or 1080 miles/hr _average_ speed! However,


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-001.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:47]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  assuming constant acceleration, the peak speed at disappearance
  would be twice this or 2160 miles/hr! Not even a VTOL Harrier
  jet (which didn't even exist until 1966) can pull that off.
  Now let's use more conservative numbers. Triple the
  disappearance time to 60 seconds or 1 minute. I did experiments
  with a target of the angular size of the Socorro object and
  tried to take contrast and sun glare into account. If it was a
  hazy day that reduced contrast and Zamora had some sun glare in
  his eyes (but had typical corrected normal acuity--remember, he
  had his glasses on again at this point), then maybe the fadeout
  distance was as low as 2.5 miles. (On the other hand, I found it
  could be as high as 8 miles, but we are trying to give the
  balloon theory every chance it can get by using the most
  conservative numbers.)
  Now we have the object traveling 2.5 miles to fadeout in 1
  minute for an _average_ speed of 150 mph, but, with constant
  acceleration, a peak speed of 300 mph. Again, even with these
  extremely conservative assumptions, the object departed at much
  too high a speed to be a balloon, even ignoring the fact that it
  would be flying into the wind. These high speeds also
  conclusively rule out other things as well, such as helicopters
  or small planes, even if we ignore another fact that Zamora
  reported that the departure was completely silent (a highly
  important detail that has nothing to do with his glasses or
  eyesight, unless his ears also fell off).
  There are other horrific problems with any balloon theory, also
  previously discussed. E.g., nobody who brings up balloon
  theories ever discusses just how difficult it is to handle a
  balloon in the wind. E.g., how easily could hoaxers control even
  a relatively small balloon of the Socorro size in even a
  relatively low wind of 10 mph (4.5 m/sec)? Balloons are like
  sails and wind exerts a lot of force on them, that goes up as
  the square of the wind speed.
  Wind pressure is given approximately by Pressure = 1/2 * air
  density * (wind speed)^2 * shape factor (drag).
  Air density at sea level is about 1.2 kg/m^3. Assume the shape
  factor is simply 1 for the ellipsoidal object. This works out to
  a pressure of about 12 Newton/m^2 or .25 pounds/ft^2 .
  Let's assume the Socorro egg-shaped object had a modest average
  cross-sectional area of 100 ft^2. Then total wind force on the
  balloon at 10 mph is only 25 pounds, which is very manageable.
  But what if the wind gusts up to 20 mph or 30 mph? Then we have
  a force of 100 pounds or 225 pounds. This is no longer so easy
  to manage. Several people would be needed to wrangle the balloon
  with cables to keep the balloon in place or to keep themselves
  from being dragged away. Why weren't they seen and how did they
  so quickly disappear without leaving a trace?
  The alternative would be to have the balloon anchored in place
  with cables. But where did the anchors magically go, again
  leaving no trace? And why didn't Zamora report the balloon
  bobbing around in the wind, whatever the scenario, as balloons
  will inevitably do?
  The hoaxers would also have to simulate a landing and a takeoff,
  by reeling in their control cables and then letting them out
  again, all while remaining completely invisible. What did they
  use? Harry Potter invisibility cloaks?
  >Has Zamora ever been re-interviewed concerning this possibility?
  >Since such Balloons are probably more common these days, has he,
  >himself, ever seen one since? If not, it might be an interesting
  >proposal to expose him to one up close to get his impressions.
  >When the hot air balloon hypothesis was being considered, I
  >naturally took more interest in it since it was not too long
  >beforehand that I had interviewed this witness - that, along
  >with my own personal experience with such balloons had naturally
  >peeked my interest.
  You can't get around these problems using a hot-air balloon with
  some control over takeoff and landing. Somebody still has to
  control the heating device to make it descend and take off
  again. Why were they invisible? (As Ray Stanford reports, Zamora
  probably got closer than 50 feet to the object, maybe 35 feet,


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-001.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:47]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  and yes, he still had his glasses on at this point, not that it
  matters much.) And you still have to keep the balloon from
  bobbing around and being blown about while it is on the ground,
  again requiring some sort of cabling with wranglers and/or
  anchors.
  If some guy was suspended from the balloon to control its
  descent and ascent (again all done invisibly), you run into
  further restrictions of physics. People have weight, and
  balloons have to have minimum sizes to support such weight. You
  can't get around this. Minimum volumes of air have to be
  displaced to create the necessary buoyancy (Archimede's
  principle). I did a calculation here on Updates some years back
  to demonstrate that even with midgets, unreasonably light
  rigging, and weightless balloon envelopes, you still need a hot
  air balloon at least 45 ft in diameter to create the necessary
  buoyancy. Real hot air balloons are much larger than this (and
  they never fly in winds over 15 mph, unless one has a death
  wish).
  The Socorro device was SUV size, not the size of a 5 story
  apartment building. No balloon this size can lift a human being,
  even a very small one. There is no possible way that Zamora
  could get that close and confuse objects that disparate in size,
  even with his glasses off, which they weren't.
  And even if one could figure out exactly how to make a balloon
  land, take off, and remain stationary on the ground in a gusty
  wind, all in a controlled way, and do this invisibibly leaving
  no trace of one's presence during the sighting or afterward, how
  does a balloon fly against the wind at hundreds and perhaps even
  thousands of miles per hour?
  Anybody who waves their hands and claims balloon explanations
  for Socorro are some sort of viable theory, just doesn't
  understand the first thing about the case or the physics of the
  situation.
  >Perhaps you can address these issues in your upcoming report. If
  >such pranksters can produce evidence of a prank as sophisticated
  >as a hot air balloon, there may be some room for discussion(?).
  >But, it's very doubtful. Otherwise, I would have to agree with
  >you that their claims are rather flimsy, mere hearsay, and do
  >not fit the facts, so far, obtained.
  Yep! That is one thing we can agree on.

  David Rudiak

  Hello Victor Golubik,
  =46rom New Mexico State Police Sergeant Sam Chavez, N.M. State
  Police Patrolman Ted V. Jordan, Socorro Undersheriff James
  Luckie, all of whom arrived at the site very quickly, and from
  others who arrives there within the next hour, we have the
  information that there was absolutely no smell of hydrocarbon
  burning or fuel residue. F.B.I. agent J. Arthur Byrnes, Jr.,
  commented that he smelled no such residue, when he visited the
  site.
  The report you received from that alleged VLA worker is totally
  without foundation, and one might consider it to be a skeptic's
  lie or maybe his letting skeptical imagination run wild.
  I suppose the inexperienced might mistake the smell of burned
  sap of the creosote bushes (ignited by the bright, burning
  diverging cone of blue 'flame' from beneath the object) as
  residue from burned fuel, but all of those who visited the site


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-003.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:49]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  within the next hour were locals used to the smell of burned
  creosote bush, and would easily have recognized it, as would I,
  a Phoenix resident in 1964.
  Furthermore, I provided Dr. Hynek with clean, new plastic vials
  with their tops textured for writing, and watched him fill the
  vials with Creosote bush twigs that had been burned in the event
  and paper that was found in locations where the reported landing
  and take-off 'exhaust' might have been directed, either during
  conceivable braking of its approach from the northeast
  (according to the tourists who reported it to Opal Grinder and
  his son at the Whiting Brothers gas station on the north side of
  town) of during lift-off prior to horizontal and then nearly
  vertical disappearance in the vicinity of the Pearlite mill ~ a
  mile west of the landing site. Hynek gave these to a laboratory
  for the government, and as he later told the USAF in his report,
  and as he told me the very next time we talked, the laboratory
  found absolutely no residue from fuel burning.
  As to the hot-air balloon idea, it simply contradicts the all
  things observed.
  Hot air balloons absolutely do not land on four widely-spaced,
  girdered legs with large, wedge-shaped landing gear (as
  evidenced by the imprints of the landing gear). The legs were
  described by Zamora as, "girder like", so that's his description
  and not mine.
  Those girder-like legs folded back into the object on lift-off.
  A balloon? Absolutely not!
  Furthermore, as Zamora demonstrated to Hynek and me on
  Wednesday, April 29, 1964 (about 4.5 days after the event), that
  as he approached the object straight-on, his prescription
  glasses with sunshades were still very much in their intended
  place on his face. He could see things quite clearly, and there
  absolutely was no gondola or fuel burner under the object. In
  fact, Zamora was quite clear that the bright blue diverging
  'exhaust' was roaring downward (and absolutely not upward as
  would the flame of burner on a balloon). Although it puzzled
  Lonnie because unlike rocked exhausts he had seen at White Sands
  Proving Ground launched, the powerful, roaring 'flame' bounced
  off nothing and seemed to knife into soil. He told Hynek and me
  in response to questioning, that by contrast to the column of
  blue light, the slightly orange periphery of the exhaust was
  stirring up some dust from the ravine bottom. As I said in the
  book, the intense blue stream of light seemingly penetrating
  into the ravine soils, suggests subatomic particles accelerated
  to relativistic velocity, for thrust. Perhaps there are other
  explanations, but one thing is for sure: It was not a plasma
  rocket exhaust. Plasma would have scattered all around and
  beyond, its ground-contact location.
  Please note that the exhaust was very clearly observed to be
  coming out of an ~ 4' orifice centered on the bottom of the ~
  18' to 20' ellipsoid. (See the carefully done illustrations in
  my Socorro book.). "Ellipsoid" is my term, not Zamora's. He
  described the object as an elongated egg-shaped thing, seemingly
  made of a light-colored metal. It absolutely was not shaped as a
  light bulb, as would be a hot-air balloon back in those days.
  Zamora walked to about 35 feet of the object, on an ~ north-
  south course, from where he got out of the patrol car. There
  was a rather brisk wind blowing out of the W-SW, according to
  both Zamora and Walter Shrode of Socorro Radio KSRC, who kept
  track of the weather for reporting it on the radio.
  So, guess what! A skeptic's hot air balloon would have to rise
  up vertically and then in total silence zip off directly against
  a stiff breeze from the opposite direction,on a perfectly
  horizontal course for ~ 1 mile, then quickly climb up almost
  vertically (as least as viewed from the landing site) and out of
  sight in how many seconds? Twenty! Well, let's play
  conservative, contradict the observer, and say it only got as
  far as the Pearlite mill in that twenty seconds.
  Guess what. That would still mean the balloon averaged 180
  miles per hour flying directly into the stiff wind, just during
  its horizontal trip to the Pearlite mill.
  Fine, but using facts and not a skeptic's imagination, on UFO


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-003.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:49]

  UpDates Brad Sparks demonstrated that the object became
  supersonic before it disappeared high in the sky.
  So according to several lawmen's sense of smell within a short
  time after the object's take-off, there was no smell-
  recognizable odor of fuel or fuel-burning residue. In fact,
  several of the lawmen told me they specifically sniffed,
  expecting to detect such, with no success.
  Hynek's samples confirmed that fact: No fuel residue of and
  kind.
  I could say a whole lot more, but, heck, I'd rather hold some
  very important facts as surprises for when on Friday, October 2,
  at 1:30 to 3:30 eastern time (according to the present
  schedule), I'm going to both slay Bragalia's ludicrous
  declaration that the Socorro object was just a college prank,
  and bury the damnable thing so that future generations, which
  tend to remember only 'headlines' can know how much bovine feces
  was being thrown at not only a well-substantiated daylight CE
  III case, but in the face of a very observant and highly
  respected 1964 public servant, Lonnie Zamora.
  In closing, I must ask each reader to think about this:. Ask

                                       Argentine Woman Visited by 14 'Extraterrestrials'
  From: Scott Corrales <lornis1.nul>
  Date: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 09:13:36 -0400
  Archived: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 12:38:33 -0400
  Subject: Argentine Woman Visited by 14 'Extraterrestrials'

  The Journal of Hispanic Ufology
  September 28, 2009


  Source: Popular On-Line
  http://www.popularonline.com.ar/nota.php?Nota=3D413256
  Date: 09.28.09
  Argentina: Woman Visited by 14 'Extraterrestrials'
  The event occurred at a house in Florencio Varela and the case
  promises to have worldwide repercussions
  They were fourteen aliens in total. Suddenly, in the middle of
  the night, they were surrounding the bed she shared with her
  husband and small daughter. They looked at her silently, and at
  no moment did she feel fear. She wanted to wake up her husband,
  to have him witness what was occurring before her very eyes, but
  the Vistors advised her telepathically that he would remain
  asleep, as he was still not yet ready. Then, the beings revealed
  all of their secrets to her during a 40 minute time period. This
  is a 'contact' experience between a young woman from the town of
  Florencio Varela and a group of aliens - a case whose mysterious
  elements have the research community on tenterhooks, and
  promises to become known worldwide.
  The entire episode forms part of an impressive investigation
  undertaken by the renowned founders of Grupo OVNI - Jorge Marron
  and Daniel Valverdi - who managed to secure an interview last
  week with the young woman, 23, who lives in a house in Florencio
  Varela along with her husband and child. "The case is currently
  under investigation and the analyses have not yet been
  completed. We are working with a multi-disciplinary team of
  experts who have contributed their manpower to unravel the case.
  As we can tell right now, it is 90% true.", Valverdi told
  Expedientes Secretos.
  'Karina' is the name of the protagonist, and the story is truly
  enthralling. Among other revelations, she said that the aliens
  visit our planet on six-month rotations, and are replaced by a
  new group. They also explained that the circles and shapes that
  appear on the fields are signals they employ among themselves,
  and finally explained that there are dozens of civilizations
  from other planets that come down and interact on Earth to study
  it (and surely to study us).
  "I never had an experience of this sort. It happened two and a
  half months ago. We were in bed - my husband, my baby and me.
  They were asleep and I was trying to fall asleep. Suddenly I
  felt lights, and when I opened my eyes there was nothing there.
  I closed my eyes again, and the same thing happened. Like a
  flash of light. I opened my eyes, but all I could see was the
  [pilot light] on the stove." explained Karina, on audio
  recordings broadcast on the Codigo OVNI radio show (FM Apuntes,
  Mondays, 22:00 hrs.)


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-005.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:50]
Argentine Woman Visited by 14 'Extraterrestrials'


  "The fourth time that this happened, I felt it closer. I opened
  my eyes and saw the beings standing beside me. I was lying
  between my husband and my daughter. I uncovered myself and sat
  up. They were around the bed, fourteen of them, all of them
  short except for one: this last one was beside my husband,
  staring at him fixedly. The small ones were all around, and
  measured 1.30 meters or so. They were slightly taller than my
  bed's sommier, which is rather tall.", said the young woman.
  In her description of the events to researches Marron and
  Valverdi, the witness explained that she tried to wake her
  husband, but was unable to do so. "I even dug my nails into him,
  but nothing. The baby was on the other side, on her stomach, but
  neither of them reacted. At that time, one of the beings told me
  not to wake up my husband, because he was not ready. I realize
  that they spoke to me telepathically, and I could answer them in
  the same way.", Karina explained.
  "They were practically alike. The only difference was the height
  of one of their number. They were skinny, with long necks and
  small heads. Their eyes were black, like almonds, slanted, and
  their mouths were also small. They had little wrinkles on their
  face and the head was oval. They had shoulders; their fingers
  were long and had no nails.", described the woman, who was
  standing beside a group of aliens who touched her hair, the
  palms of her hands and lifted one of her arms.'
  "Five of them were on the left, four facing me and five on the
  right side of the bed. I was the only one who wasn't paralyzed.
  We communicated for some 40 minutes, mouths closed. That is to
  say, I spoke by my mouth didn't move. I managed to touch one of
  the little ones, and he touched my face. I just felt the need to
  touch him, and in fact I felt peaceful in their company. I never
  had the impression that they could harm my daughter or any one
  of us.", Karina insists.
  "Suddenly he made a movement (sic).", said the witness. "He
  touched me with two fingers, and finally brushed a finger along
  my forehead. They told me they had come to give me answers about
  a black book (the Bible) and that what I was thinking was in
  fact true. When I asked them a question, they told me
  everything, with details."
  In her conversation with the researchers, Karina stated: "The
  tall one was looking at my husband all the time. His head was
  slightly smaller than the others, because the small ones had
  rather larger heads. It felt like touching a baby's skin - they
  had little wrinkles, but I couldn't feel them when I touched one
  of them. The skin was cold and the color was odd, neither white
  nor gray. They had a skin tone that doesn't exist here."
  -----
  Translation (c) 2009, S. Corrales, IHU
  Special thanks to Guillermo Gimenez, Planeta UFO, and Jorge
  Marr=F3n and Daniel Valverdi, Grupo OVNI)

Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast




                                                          UFO Updates

                                               'Its All Here In Black & White'
    Location: UFOUpDatesList.Com > 2009 > Sep > Sep 28

                                     Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  From: Ed Gehrman <egehrman.nul>
  Date: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 10:16:14 -0700
  Archived: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 14:13:56 -0400
  Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  >From: Don Ledger <dledger.nul>
  >To: UFO UpDates - Toronto <post.nul>
  >Date: Sun, 27 Sep 2009 21:27:27 -0300
  >Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  >Suggest you read the book Ray wrote, A Socorro 'Saucer' In a
  >Pentagon Pantry. It's the best investigation into that event.
  >Those who haven't read it are proceeding without any real
  >information.
  Don,
  I have read Ray's book and all David's arguments. I still think
  the "bean" is a better explanation. The best feature of the
  "bean" story is that by following the path of the object as it
  flew away, we should still be able to find the discarded body of
  the "Barn" machine, which should still be in one of the arroyos
  west of the sighting.
  Lonnie and the Bean
  http://www.weirdload.com/nm-ufo.html
  "I recently read a curious little book entitled Flying Saucers -
  Key to Creation? by a 'George H. Eastland'. Despite the title,
  there's no channeling, contactee lore or New Age blather.
  Instead, this gentleman seems to be an old aerospace engineer
  who's seen a few things and thought long and hard about them.
  Very nuts and bolts type of guy, but with a surprising amount of
  insightful and intelligent speculation about the various ways
  flying saucers could work.
  In this thought-provoking book, he tells an amazing story as a
  cautionary tale that things are not always as they seem and that
  there is more than one kind of UFO out there. Illustrated with
  diagrams from bar napkins and replete with fascinating technical
  details, the yarn was supposedly related to him by a biker
  called "Tea-Mex," whom he met at a now long-vanished bar during
  a motorcycle rally in New Hampshire many years ago. They fell to
  talking about motorcycles, and thence other machines, which led
  to flying saucers, and then the guy causally remarked that he'd
  built one once.
  A do-it-yourself flying saucer
  With a little prodding, Tea-Mex explained he was a machinist who
  at one time hung out with a bunch of fellows from some unnamed
  contractor to the military in central New Mexico. (Most likely
  involved with White Sands, because not only were they able to
  get custom-milled aluminum and steel parts, but practical advice
  from on-site aviation experts. But that's getting ahead of the
  story.)
  Anyway, these guys started playing around with the design of a
  engine described in Popular Mechanics which consisted of two
  pistons on opposite ends of a rod shuttling back and forth
  between combustion chambers. Since the pistons didn't do
  anything but make a lot of hot compressed exhaust gas, it was


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-007.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:51]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  considered merely an impractical toy.
  After melting a version cobbled out of old lawnmowers, the boys
  got serious. They machined parts at work, hiding them in a
  dumpster for later retrieval - "involuntary contributions from
  the aircraft industry" as Tea-Mex put it. By the time they were
  through, they basically had a vertically-mounted jet engine that
  spun like a gyroscope, powered by propane. Once they mounted a
  big fan atop it to increase the cooling airflow, the lift,
  combined with the thrust of the exhaust, was enough to allow the
  device to actually fly.
  Of course, nothing for it then but to build a light frame with
  fixed landing gear, put a couple of chairs in for the pilot and
  engineer, and encase the frame in white aircraft aluminum. One
  sheet had an old logo on it which they attempted to paint over
  with abstract "characters" in red. They called their egg-shaped
  contraption "the Bean" because it looked somewhat like a lima
  bean mounted on four toothpicks.
  Being essentially a really big flying gyro, the ship was quite
  tricky to maneuver, requiring two people, who dressed in jump
  suits with motorcycle helmets. On one test flight going into a
  strong headwind, the guys had to land abruptly in an arroyo
  right off a highway. But being spotted and chased by a local
  cop, they quickly hopped back in and took off again.
  They weren't quite so lucky this time and some distance away
  suffered a crash landing that wrecked the Bean, though they were
  unhurt. But they were stuck: they couldn't patent or sell the
  damn thing because all the parts had been scrounged or otherwise
  ill-gotten. Nor, having been totally unlicensed, dare they come
  forward. So they secretly trashed the ship but didn't have the
  heart to junk the engine, which is still quietly rusting in
  somebody's garage somewhere out there. The end.
  The biker finished his beer and rode off. Tea-Mex had never
  mentioned the policeman by name, though one of the guys had said
  he'd recognized him. Nor do any other identifying names
  whatsoever appear in the story.
  But in all the annals of ufology, this tale could relate to one
  and only one case: the world-famous sighting by Socorro Police
  Sergeant Lonnie Zamora, who on April 24, 1964, saw and pursued a
  craft landing in a gully near Socorro. Two occupants emerged
  briefly but hastily re-entered the craft, which then took off
  with a flaming roar, leaving behind some of the best trace
  evidence ever collected.
  The incident got a great deal of national attention. The head of
  Project Blue Book admitted that it was the "best-documented case
  on record." The story of the Bean fits the details exactly, even
  down to the funny symbol on the side of the craft and its near
  collision with a green Cadillac on the highway just before the
  landing, details which are not that widely known.
  And it sounds very plausible, especially with all the
  engineering detail in the full story. Indeed, people at the time
  wondered if it wasn't some NASA lunar-landing gizmo being tested
  at nearby White Sands that had seriously gone astray. This was
  around the time the first prototypes of the LM were being
  experimented with there, after all. Plus the craft made a lot of
  noise and even started a fire in the bushes with its exhaust,
  which is not typical of most flying saucers.
  Apparently, the vessel may have been a bit more astray from the
  military-industrial complex than anyone had thought, but not
  from anywhere near as far away as most imagined."

  Ed

  I don't think anyone said that these ETs had a message for the
  world. Rather, any communication seemed to be specifically
  intended for the woman involved, since she was told that even
  her sleeping husband was not ready to meet them. Communication
  was telepathic, further suggesting that it was meant for her
  alone.
  This kind of encounter with telepathic communication is
  consistent with it occurring via the woman's unconscious. As in


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m28-009.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:52]
Re: Argentine Woman Visited by 14

  other cases, such as in shamanic rituals and abductions, this
  channel appears to be accessible only under special conditions,
  perhaps involving an altered state of consciousness.
  I have the impression that communications like these from the
  'beyond' are always personally meaningful. Maybe that's the
  nature of the place. The message, if and when it is disclosed,
  is likely to be most relevant to the woman herself since it was
  mediated by her personal consciousness.
  For those of us on a different path, I expect it will feel like
  reading someone else's personal mail.

  William

  ago] but the symbol does not imply the German connotation in
  1870 but probably has some religious concept and has shown up in
  records from China, India, Europe, Egyption hieroglyphs and even
  in the North American Indian lore.
  See:
  http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swastika#Archaeological_record
  The swastika was used by the Nazis but was not invented by
  them. It apparently was one of the symbols in a church which
  Hitler attended in Austria during his youth. Some think he
  pirated its shape for his own use.
  One has to wonder, however why in 1870 someone would have wasted
  a photographic plate on a half-smoke cigar laying in the snow.
  My suspicions are that this photograph is more recent than 1870.
  One reason I'm suspicious is that the Nazis were the only ones I
  could find who displayed the swastika standing on one of it's

  points [as it looks to be on the cigar] rather than flat.

  >flew away, we should still be able to find the discarded body of
  >the "Barn" machine, which should still be in one of the arroyos
  >west of the sighting.
  >Lonnie and the Bean
  >http://www.weirdload.com/nm-ufo.html
  >"I recently read a curious little book entitled Flying Saucers -
  >Key to Creation? by a 'George H. Eastland'. Despite the title,
  >there's no channeling, contactee lore or New Age blather.
  >Instead, this gentleman seems to be an old aerospace engineer
  >who's seen a few things and thought long and hard about them.
  >Very nuts and bolts type of guy, but with a surprising amount of
  >insightful and intelligent speculation about the various ways
  >flying saucers could work.
  >
  >In this thought-provoking book, he tells an amazing story as a
  >cautionary tale that things are not always as they seem and that
  >there is more than one kind of UFO out there. Illustrated with
  >diagrams from bar napkins and replete with fascinating technical
  >details, the yarn was supposedly related to him by a biker
  >called "Tea-Mex," whom he met at a now long-vanished bar during
  >a motorcycle rally in New Hampshire many years ago. They fell to
  >talking about motorcycles, and thence other machines, which led
  >to flying saucers, and then the guy causally remarked that he'd
  >built one once.
  <snip>
  George H. Eastland being an aviation engineer should have
  recognized the Dyna-Cam engine invented in the US back in the
  early 1940s but earlier examples invented by many countries goes
  back to 1906 [wobble plate type engines, etc.]. The engine I'm
  referring to, the Dyna-Cam appears to originate from a design by
  the Blazer brothers, who worked for Studebaker in 1916. They
  sold the rights to Karl Herrmann, Studebaker's head of
  engineering. He developed it over many years, taking out a
  patent in 1941; see US patent 2237989.
  See this website:


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m29-003.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:53]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast


  http://tinyurl.com/yb32wxf
  The pistons shuttle back and forth in a revolver pistol type
  setup using lobes on the side of the connecting rods to drive
  the central crankshaft. The beauty of it was it only had about 7
  moving parts. It was too late, however, to shift from
  conventional radials and watercooled engines like the Merlin so
  the engine was used instead in torpedos and driven by compressed
  air.
  The Dyna-Cam came back in the 1980s and was produced in small
  numbers but never adopted by aviation. It could run as low as
  75RPM and was very quiet, practically vibration free, small and
  efficient. It's still out there but as far as I know has never
  been certified for certified, factory-built aircraft; probably
  because that costs multi-millions of dollars.
  The Italians invented a 'jet' engine back in the late 1930s. Its
  compressors [the fans] were drive by a conventional recip.
  engine, then additional fuel was introduced into the combustion
  stage. But that required two stages, two types of fuel adding
  more weight and was less efficient than just sticking a prop on
  the original recip. engine.
  I don't give much credence to the hypothesis as proposed by
  Eastland. The story in Mechanics Illustrated was about the Dyna-
  Cam and the free piston engine which produces a jet exhaust.
  Could have been the latter that was referred to.
  But let's not forget about control surfaces for this Lima Bean
  which in this case would have to had to be additional thrusters
  of some type plus something pushing the thing as well. Now you
  are into some major engineering. Where was this tested. They
  must have done something to insure that they didn't kill
  themselves. They apparently had no flying experience. I think
  this Lima Bean 'craft' is a gag.

  Don Ledger

Pelicanists 1947

                                                                Pelicanists 1947
  From: Jerome Clark <jkclark.nul>
  Date: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 16:52:37 -0400
  Archived: Tue, 29 Sep 2009 07:45:00 -0400
  Subject: Pelicanists 1947

  Nevada State Journal
  [Reno]
  July 13, 1947

  Many People Can Explain Those Discs
  Each of Them Wants Thousand Dollar Reward
  CHICAGO, July 12. (U.P.) - Hundreds of amateur scientists
  swamped the office of a Chicago industrialist today with letters
  "explaining" the "flying saucers" which have been whizzing
  through the sky.
  Each of the writers wanted a $1,000 reward offered by E. J.
  Culligan for a "flying saucer" in hand - or at least the correct
  explanation of the things people say they have been seeing.
  Culligan, who was the first of several persons to offer rewards
  for capture of discs, said he'd received just about every answer
  in the book - ranging from red corpuscles on the eyeballs to
  reflections from melting icebergs.
  "I have never thought so many people could have so many
  different explanations for the things," Culligan said.
  He even got a score of telegrams from persons who wanted to be
  first with the "inside dope" on the discs.
  W. R. Dodds, Brooklyn, N.Y., wired the red corpuscle theory.
  Edward Jackson, Reading, Pa., telegraphed that the disks really
  were bubbles from the wire dimestore bubble makers.
  "Icebergs"
  Jim Grill, Houston, Tex., wrote that the discs were reflections
  from melting icebergs. Here's how he said the phenomena occurs
  [sic]: When the fresh water in the bergs mixes with the salt
  water, oily rings of color are formed. The rings reflect
  against the iceberg, and, in turn, against the sky.
  "As this iceberg disappears, so will the discs or saucers,"
  Grill said.
  T. A. Marshall of St. Louis was convinced the discs were nothing
  but "phosphorous silica" caught in huge wind drafts. He said
  he's all set to build a machine to make the things, just to
  prove his point.
  From Los Angeles, Walter Hilgadiack sent three possible
  theories.
  The saucers, he said, either were the reflection of the sun on
  the moon, the army signal corps trying to contact the moon by
  radar - or maybe even a return signal from the moon.
  John A. Bruckner, Brooklyn, N.Y., a student of hypnosis, said
  "one of our enemies" had hypnotized the whole nation into
  thinking it was being bombarded by discs "to create a form of
  panic in this atomic age."


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m29-004.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:54]
Pelicanists 1947


  Culligan said he'd read every letter that has come into his
  office.
  "Very interesting," he said.                  "But I'm still waiting for the
  answer."

  From: William Treurniet <wtreurniet.nul>
  Date: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 21:01:37 -0400

  ><snip>
  >'Karina' is the name of the protagonist, and the story is truly
  >enthralling. Among other revelations, she said that the aliens
  >visit our planet on six-month rotations, and are replaced by a
  >new group. They also explained that the circles and shapes that
  >appear on the fields are signals they employ among themselves,
  >and finally explained that there are dozens of civilizations
  >from other planets that come down and interact on Earth to study
  >it (and surely to study us).
  ><snip>
  I found interesting this tidbit that "the circles and shapes
  that appear on the fields are signals they employ among
  themselves". This comment coincides with some speculative
  research I started a couple of years ago. I proposed then that
  some of the crop formations appeared to be symbols denoting
  progress on a journey. I suggested that the formations are
  interstellar messages using as a channel the substrate in which
  physical reality is represented. Encoding a message in this
  channel would result in corresponding physical changes in the
  environment that could appear as crop formations. The crop
  formations would be an incidental side effect of the
  communication, which allows us to monitor the conversation among
  the aliens.
  I selected a sequence of formations from 1996 onward that is
  consistent with a message context of 'expedition itinerary'
  suggested by a few key formations. The messages represented
  states one might encounter from the beginning to the end of an
  interstellar journey. The extracted message sequence is
  presented in "An alien itinerary decoded from crop formations"
  located at:
  http://www.treurniet.ca/Itin/.
  The messages were interpreted to say that a large group of ETs
  have recently arrived at our world. They describe vacating a
  home world, entering a stargate, leaving a stargate, flying
  through space, approaching gravity wells, and surrounding the
  earth. There is internal consistency across formations. For


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m29-007.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:55]
Re: Argentine Woman Visited by 14

  example, the checkerboard pattern appears to represent the alien
  home world, a bird icon appears to represent one or more mother
  ships, a wing-like structure seems to represent smaller ships,
  and a sphere accompanied by the wing-like structures appears to
  represent a higher-dimensional space. The logical sequence and
  internal consistency argue that the messages are related and
  were authored by an intelligent agent.
  The weakness of the methodology for extracting messages is that
  it involves subjective judgments. One can argue that I only got
  what I was looking for and so the message sequence was a self-
  fulfilling prophecy. However, there are model-based filters such
  as the Kalman filter that are conceptually similar. One begins
  with an expectation of what is to be found in order to ignore
  noise in the signal. So, yes, if the filter model is wrong, one
  will get output that is meaningless. Conversely, however, if the
  output of the filter makes sense then one can argue that the
  model is correct.
  The message sequence also includes a paired sequence of
  formations and a particular combination of symbols within one of
  the pair that is thought to represent passage through something
  like a stargate. This spatio-temporal complex appeared once in
  2001, once in 2008, and twice in 2009. If this interpretation is
  correct, the crop formations tell us of at least four arrivals
  to our region of 3D space.
  The idea that at least some of the crop formations are inter-
  alien communications did not appear to catch on among crop
  circle devotees. I'm somewhat heartened that someone, apparently
  the aliens themselves, seem to agree with this interpretation.
  But just because there is evidence that some formations are
  "signals they employ among themselves" does not negate the
  possibility that other formations are meant for us. We just need
  to figure out the correct message context.

  William

  Basically we have 'evidence' on par with a 'true story about a
  fictitious character'. And it sounds very unlikely to me that
  they would "invent" (?) a vertical jet that would spin like a
  gyroscope. If they did have a gyro, with its spin axis
  vertical, then it would indeed be tricky to operate. And it
  would require sideways-exhausting rockets (or propellors) in
  order to make it travel sideways. And it wouldn't be lightweight
  if designed to travel very far because of the high rate of fuel
  use to get the appropriate thrust.

  I don't buy it until someone comes up with the hard evidence
  which, it is suggested, should exist "out there" on the desert
  near Socorro.

                                     Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  From: Gerald O'Connell <goc.nul>
  Date: Tue, 29 Sep 2009 13:14:59 +0100
  Archived: Tue, 29 Sep 2009 12:01:00 -0400
  Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  >From: Don Ledger <dledger.nul>
  >To: UFO UpDates - Toronto <post.nul>
  >Date: Mon, 28 Sep 2009 18:32:26 -0300
  >Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  <snip>
  >But let's not forget about control surfaces for this Lima Bean
  >which in this case would have to had to be additional thrusters
  >of some type plus something pushing the thing as well. Now you
  >are into some major engineering. Where was this tested. They
  >must have done something to insure that they didn't kill
  >themselves. They apparently had no flying experience. I think
  >this Lima Bean 'craft' is a gag.
  I think you've hit the nail on the head right there, Don.
  Looking at the reported performance characteristics, it simply
  doesn't fit an experimental garage prototype. You talk about
  'major engineering': enough basic stability to just get off the
  ground, achieve any kind of controlled movement, and then come
  back to earth without destroying the entire mechanism would be
  challenge enough. And then go back to the actual reported
  movement and you have your answer.
  I had the good fortune to know the late Len Cramp, and he was a
  practical genius when it came to experimental flying machines.
  Having learned from Len how much work is involved in achieving
  basic stability and manoeuvrability at the prototype stage, all
  this without even scaling up to the real thing, I can assure you
  that It just doesn't stack up.
  The tale illustrates another point: it seems that there is a
  direct correlation between the solidity/perplexity of the
  sighting and the inherent absurdity of the 'explanations' put
  forward to make it go away.
  --
  Gerald O'Connell
  http://www.onlyport.com

  >>you that their claims are rather flimsy, mere hearsay, and do
  >>not fit the facts, so far, obtained.
  >Yep! That is one thing we can agree on.

  Hi David,
  Yes, I understand the physics of the situation very well but I
  also understand how witnesses can defy the laws of physics!
  Evidently, you haven't yet crossed that fundamental threshold. I
  might suggest you start investigating fresh cases and begin to
  get a feel for all the potential "real world" problems that
  exist.
  We can often vacillate around seemingly fundamental issues
  because the juxtaposition of both weak and strong evidential
  components can be weighed differently - each affecting the
  underpinnings of the other.
  Some of Zamora's testimony and others:
  "...The two legs were at the bottom of the object, slanted
  outwards to the ground." Zamora then got closer to the object,
  got out of his car, heard a loud roar, saw a flame, ran, bumped
  his leg, lost his glasses, and kept on going. He saw the object
  fly up, and move 10 to 15 feet above the ground, and then leave
  the area "traveling very fast." He radioed his dispatcher to
  look out his window for 'an object... it looks like a balloon.'
  Nearby, the bushes were still smoldering. News reports in the
  local paper, El Defensor Chieftain, also mentioned "an
  unidentified tourist" who remarked about how " aircraft flew low
  around here," and that the strange object was a 'funny-looking
  helicopter, if that's what it was'."
  As we can see, both helicopter and balloon are mentioned by
  witnesses. And if Zamora saw a small landed helicopter it might
  be described as the size of a Volkswagen since it deviates from
  the size of the smaller vehicle he described and focused on


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m29-012.shtml[11/12/2011 19:27:59]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  later. Might there have be two vehicles involved? Could the
  occupants have departed in a small helicopter tethering the
  device? He lost his glasses before it took off, his vision was
  poor, and he was already contaminated by having testified
  publicly on a radio program (with a showman host) before he was
  interviewed by others - where the townsfolk describe that
  interview as him having been force-fed.
  Should we now lead in a debate about his vision problems and the
  fact his sun glasses skewed his color perception. That he
  actually lost his glasses during the sighting! That in an early
  interview he mentions the possibility that the crew did not
  board the vehicle. That there is evidence of a tethered surveyor
  test flight on April 24 at 7:45 AM... along with another test
  vehicle designed by Hughes which was tethered and self
  sufficient. That 37 companies competed for contracts for the
  surveyor programs and the like.
  As we can see quite plainly, we have another problem with star-
  truck advocates getting emotionally involved with cases and not
  willing or unable to bring up other aspects. But, let's hope
  that our own narrow mindedness doesn't necessarily lure us into
  single-minded solutions - boxed in without ability nor room to
  expand.
  Once I saw Ray's post, my only interest was in an exchange with
  the actual investigator. An investigator who actually spoke with
  witnesses and was on the ground taking in the entire experience
  and exchanging with the witnesses.
  I've learned quite convincingly that many cases have both major
  and minor flaws and it is the willingness of the investigator to
  discuss them that speaks well for them and the entire case -
  though it is often most unappreciated by both sides of the
  debate.
  The following are interesting reads:
  http://tinyurl.com/yh7b39g
  http://forufo.blogspot.com
  http://www.nmsr.org/socorro.htm
  I'm afraid I have no interest in proving the world is flat in
  today's climate. Though you are quite capable of leading others
  in that direction.
  Can I once find an investigator willing to look at both angles
  with vigor and enthusiasm? Can we honestly say this sounds more
  like their technology and doesn't resound with aspects of our
  own?
  See my response to Don Ledger and Ray as well...                          no need to
  further repeat myself...

  golubik

  He was quite clear and emphatic. I did not bring up the case
  initially but was asking about his relationship with Barney
  Barnett. He was a BLM worker (not a VLA worker): that's the
  Bureau of Land Management.
  >I suppose the inexperienced might mistake the smell of burned
  >sap of the creosote bushes (ignited by the bright, burning
  >diverging cone of blue 'flame' from beneath the object) as
  >residue from burned fuel, but all of those who visited the site
  >within the next hour were locals used to the smell of burned
  >creosote bush, and would easily have recognized it, as would I,
  >a Phoenix resident in 1964.
  I lived in New Mexico (Roswell) and Phoenix. And creosote was
  exactly what I was thinking though I didn't want to suggest a
  specific plant.
  >Furthermore, I provided Dr. Hynek with clean, new plastic vials
  >with their tops textured for writing, and watched him fill the
  >vials with Creosote bush twigs that had been burned in the event
  >and paper that was found in locations where the reported landing
  >and take-off 'exhaust' might have been directed, either during
  >conceivable braking of its approach from the northeast
  >(according to the tourists who reported it to Opal Grinder and
  >his son at the Whiting Brothers gas station on the north side of
  >town) of during lift-off prior to horizontal and then nearly
  >vertical disappearance in the vicinity of the Pearlite mill ~ a
  >mile west of the landing site. Hynek gave these to a laboratory
  >for the government, and as he later told the USAF in his report,
  >and as he told me the very next time we talked, the laboratory
  >found absolutely no residue from fuel burning.
  That is not surprising given the source - If we are to be
  strictly equalized of the potential for both AF deception and
  the limits of detection of chemical residue under these
  circumstances. If a bush had been burning for a while, I
  certainly wouldn't expect to find residue from a portion of it's
  surface no longer in existence? I would have been more clever in
  obtaining my samples which clearly has to be demonstrated. And,
  wouldn't a burning bush (creosote bush) already contain a
  flavorful variety of hydrocarbons? I would have collected other
  samples for comparison - that were burned by various fuels. I'd
  have to see that analysis which I could then readily evaluate
  with confidence compared with the total. On the face of what I
  now know, I'd have to say it was greatly flawed.
  >As to the hot-air balloon idea, it simply contradicts the all
  >things observed.
  I brought it up simply because it contained certain elements
  that I would rather you could eliminate rather than myself. Why
  I jumped when I saw your post. Evidently, Zamora used the
  balloon word and another witness used the helicopter word: see
  my other post.
  >Hot air balloons absolutely do not land on four widely-spaced,
  >girdered legs with large, wedge-shaped landing gear (as
  >evidenced by the imprints of the landing gear). The legs were
  >described by Zamora as, "girder like", so that's his description
  >and not mine.
  Since he described it as large as a Volkswagen and later rather
  diminutively, I often wondered whether there were two vehicles
  involved. He was interviewed on public radio prior to your
  interview with him and we have to consider the possibility of
  contamination and alteration of the facts. Several of the
  townsfolk voluntarily said to me that he was force fed to say
  certain things as a result of that first radio interview - their
  words not mine! Hard to turn around a story in another direction
  if you're already visibly on public record saying something
  else. Let's also admit he had vision problems and was not tested
  for visual acuity that I'm aware of - again a Hynek
  investigative flaw?
  >Those girder-like legs folded back into the object on lift-off.
  >A balloon? Absolutely not!
  >Furthermore, as Zamora demonstrated to Hynek and me on
  >Wednesday, April 29, 1964 (about 4.5 days after the event), that
  >as he approached the object straight-on, his prescription

  >glasses with sunshades were still very much in their intended
  >place on his face. He could see things quite clearly, and there
  >absolutely was no gondola or fuel burner under the object. In
  >fact, Zamora was quite clear that the bright blue diverging
  >'exhaust' was roaring downward (and absolutely not upward as
  >would the flame of burner on a balloon). Although it puzzled
  >Lonnie because unlike rocked exhausts he had seen at White Sands
  >Proving Ground launched, the powerful, roaring 'flame' bounced
  >off nothing and seemed to knife into soil. He told Hynek and me
  >in response to questioning, that by contrast to the column of
  >blue light, the slightly orange periphery of the exhaust was
  >stirring up some dust from the ravine bottom. As I said in the
  >book, the intense blue stream of light seemingly penetrating
  >into the ravine soils, suggests subatomic particles accelerated
  >to relativistic velocity, for thrust. Perhaps there are other
  >explanations, but one thing is for sure: It was not a plasma
  >rocket exhaust. Plasma would have scattered all around and
  >beyond, its ground-contact location.
  Since I'm a scientist we can get into that if you wish. I don't
  understand how anyone would not have retained important samples.
  So, regardless of the extraordinary claims, I'm not yet
  impressed by the Hynek investigation effort.
  >Please note that the exhaust was very clearly observed to be
  >coming out of an ~ 4' orifice centered on the bottom of the ~
  >18' to 20' ellipsoid. (See the carefully done illustrations in
  >my Socorro book.). "Ellipsoid" is my term, not Zamora's. He
  >described the object as an elongated egg-shaped thing, seemingly
  >made of a light-colored metal. It absolutely was not shaped as a

  >conservative, contradict the observer, and say it only got as
  >far as the Pearlite mill in that twenty seconds.
  Thanks for the wind data which could, evidently, still be

  >Guess what. That would still mean the balloon averaged 180

  I'm not certain under what circumstances timing was undertaken?
  And, wasn't Zamora with out glasses at this point? Is that data
  from more than one witness?
  >Fine, but using facts and not a skeptic's imagination, on UFO
  >UpDates Brad Sparks demonstrated that the object became
  >supersonic before it disappeared high in the sky.

  >fifty miles away from the proving grounds they used 4 years
  >after Socorro.
  >Suggest you read the book Ray wrote, A Socorro 'Saucer' In a
  >Pentagon Pantry. It's the best investigation into that event.
  >Those who haven't read it are proceeding without any real
  >information.
  >Don ledger
  Hi Don,
  When you actual do ground investigations and rewind events, one
  can discover both major and minor flaws. An investigator's
  willingness to discuss them often speaks equally about the
  sturdiness of the case itself. And, I'm always readily willing
  to discuss both in the pursuit of truth. Therefore, you would
  never have to pry it out of me. I'm also equally capable of
  discussing the positives - as I recently defended a SIP MUFON


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m29-014.shtml[11/12/2011 19:28:04]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  case the was incorrectly debunked by them.
  An example of ground research:
  Missiles were actually launched from Datil, New Mexico and White
  Sands didn't want it advertised. There was an actual radar
  tower built there in the mid to late 1940's and was one of the
  first. And guess what? I found the man who was stationed there
  (later became a Texas Instrument manager). Not even the
  scientists that worked in Socorro knew about it for over 50
  years. Not even CB Moore knew about it and he lived there! The
  foundation of the building is still there and the locals were
  still reluctant to talk about it. As a result of such
  experiences, I'm always open to the discovery process, even
  years later.
  The radar was one of three constructed in a triangle - used
  during the early development of our first missile programs. (The
  other two were located in other parts of the state.) But, good
  luck finding it mentioned in the history books. I also found
  witnesses (two brothers) that did see a similar radio controlled
  LEM-type test vehicle in the Socorro area in the mid 1960's and
  when I spoke with them it was not at all in reference with the
  Socorro case.
  When I interviewed the BLM (Bureau of Land Management) worker
  (not VLA worker) it was not about the Socorro case though he had
  brought it up without prompting and discussed the smell of
  burning fuel with the Zamora case. He got there before the
  others arrived. That is one other reason I brought up the wind
  direction - Depending upon ones approach to the scene of the
  incident (a brisk wind was acknowledged?) some may smell it and
  others would not.
  The towns people recall Zamora's first interview on local radio,
  which is available, and felt he was influenced to say certain
  things in the excitement of his first public interview
  processes. Therefore, he may have been contaminated before
  anyone else interviewed him. One of the reasons I became more
  interested in alternative explanations and why they could
  suddenly gain some ground with me.
  I also interviewed the host who conducted that interview. He was
  quite the showman. Unfortunately, that is what the locals
  considered when listening to his show and knew Zamora as well.
  Evidently, that host had many trysts with local young men (his
  self admission)- And, why, I believe his radio station was
  eventually burned to the ground... though he would tell another
  story and twist it in with the Zamora case and government
  conspiracy.
  See my other post to Rudiak and Ray...
  golubik

  You left out the part where Zamora reported complete silence
  once the object had lifted out of the arroyo. The departure at
  high speed was in silence, perhaps the most significant part of
  his tetimony. (Of course, knowing the way you operate, you will
  probably label it one of those "weak evidential components" and
  give zero weight to it.)
  In the real world, complete silence rules out helicopters, jets,
  rockets, internal combustion engines, etc., etc. What we have
  instead is a silent propulsion system capable of high
  acceleration and achieving high speed.
  But just how fast was "traveling very fast"? That's where the
  physics comes in, which Viktor ignores, I guess another one of
  those "weak evidential components". As I wrote previously, if
  Ray Stanford's estimate of a 20 second departure (increased from
  Zamora's 10 second estimate) to fadeout in the distance (about 6
  miles by Zamora's estimate), then the craft _averaged_ over 1000
  mph in its departure, though its peak speed could have been much
  higher, over 2000 mph if there were constant acceleration.
  Incidentally, I've done visual experiments which demonstrate


http://www.ufoupdateslist.com/2009/sep/m30-001.shtml[11/12/2011 19:28:05]
Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  that Zamora's fadeout distance of 6 miles is quite reasonable
  given standard visual acuity. I found that it could have been as
  high as 8 miles with very good seeing conditions or as low as
  2.5 miles with a lot of haze and sun glare. But Zamora was
  certainly in the ball park in terms of feasible fadeout
  distance.
  So if you want to push some "helicopter" theory, you not only
  need to ignore the silence testimony, you need to somehow
  decrease the speed by absurd amounts, say triple the departure
  time and assume a bare minimum fadeout distance, let's say 2
  miles. This cuts the average speed by a factor of 9, down to 120
  mph, though the peak speed would have to be substantially higher
  than this, perhaps 240 mph with constant acceleration. (Unless
  Viktor also wants to make another absurd assumption that the
  "helicopter" instantly achieved 120 mph speed).
  That's getting pretty close to impossible for our magical silent

  off, but being towed by the helicopter, without ever explaining

  >Could the

  >device?
  Yes, the silent helicopter that left no trace evidence behind,
  such as landing skid marks.
  >He lost his glasses before it took off, his vision was >poor,
  This is the typical "lost his glasses" red herring and also a
  highly inaccurate rendition of what Zamora actually reported. He
  did NOT lose his glasses BEFORE it took off. (Show us Viktor
  where he said that.) He approached CLOSE to the object with his
  glasses on. When the object started to take off, he was
  terrified, thinking it might explode. He turned around and
  started scrambling back out of the arroyo, shooting looks over
  his shoulder, glasses still on. When he got back to his car on
  the rim of the arroyo, he bumped into it, and at this point lost
  his glasses. He ran beyond his car and dived for cover, turned
  around and watched the object lift up out of the arroyo, then go
  completely silent (what do his glasses have to do with this
  part?), and then depart horizontally at high speed.
  He then ran back to his car, quickly retrieved his glasses, and
  watched the object leave the scene and fadeout in the distance,
  _with his glasses on_.
  Time without glasses--maybe 20 seconds. Effect on critical
  details of the sighting--zero.
  It is pretty clear that Viktor and many others have no idea what
  visual acuity is measuring. As I've repeatedly pointed out,
  traffic cops have to have at least 20/100 acuity without
  correction to pass their physicals. If 20/20 is standard acuity,
  20/100 means they can just read a line of letters at 20 feet
  that is five times as large as a 20/20 line.
  20/20 letters are about 9 mm high at 20 feet. (Designed so that
  width of black and white portions of letters are all 1 minute of
  arc wide) Thus 20/100 letters are 45 mm or about 2 inches high
  at 20 feet. If Zamora dashed 100 feet away where he dived for
  cover, then 20/100 acuity means he could just discern letters
  about 10 inches high made up of lines about 2 inches wide.
  But the oval object was 15-20 feet long, and perhaps half as
  wide. Would he have any problems seeing that? No, of course not.
  Visual acuity is a measure of how well we see fine detail, not
  the gross stuff. So the edges of the object would have been a
  little fuzzy, but he would have had no trouble seeing the much
  bigger details, such as the entire object itself.
  That's why I say bringing up his vision without his glasses on
  is a red herring. Nothing critical in his observations was
  affected by his glasses being off. And, of course, his visual
  acuity, glasses or no glasses, had no effect on his ability to
  detect the object going completely silent as it made its
  departure, the critical detail that you'll notice Viktor never
  brings up.
  >and he was already contaminated by having testified

                                          'Diary' On Early Days Of UFO Phenomenon
  From: Steve Sawyer <stevesaw.nul>
  Date: Tue, 29 Sep 2009 15:19:44 -0700
  Archived: Wed, 30 Sep 2009 09:02:36 -0400
  Subject: 'Diary' On Early Days Of UFO Phenomenon

  Saturday Night Uforia Diary On Early Days Of UFO Phenomenon
  Hi, Errol/List,
  I wanted to recommend List members check out a very interesting
  weekly "diary", Saturday Night Uforia, on the Daily Kos website,
  posted each Saturday evening by a user calling themselves 'two
  roads'
  They've posted a lengthy series of articles - over 50 so far -
  on the early days of the UFO phenomenon, particularly the 1947
  though 1952 period. The articles specifically detail and include
  voluminous original documentation from a variety of sources.
  They provide a rather unbiased and fairly well-developed
  examination of this seminal period in the history of ufology,
  the US government's role within it and the various
  personalities, such as Dr. J. Allen Hynek, who were active in
  this era.
  The most interesting aspect of this series of articles is how
  the author, 'two roads', has drawn on so many different sources
  of original documentation, which are replicated within the
  articles, to weave such an interesting historical presentation
  of the time period covered.
  It is also interesting for the number and quality of comments
  from various users appended to each article, and the author's
  feedback to these comments. Included are various links to other
  source docs.
  I highly recommend it, both for newbies and old-hands interested
  in early modern-era ufo history.
  See:
  http://two-roads.dailykos.com/

  Steve

                                     Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  From: Gerald O'Connell <goc.nul>
  Date: Wed, 30 Sep 2009 12:42:15 +0100
  Archived: Wed, 30 Sep 2009 11:35:08 -0400
  Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast

  >From: David Rudiak <drudiak.nul>
  >To: UFO UpDates - Toronto <post.nul>
  >Date: Tue, 29 Sep 2009 11:34:18 -0700
  >Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  >>From: Viktor Golubik <Diverge247.nul>
  >>To: post.nul
  >>Date: Tue, 29 Sep 2009 10:44:49 EDT
  >>Subject: Re: Ray Stanford's Comments To UFO Iconoclast
  <snip>
  >>Can I once find an investigator willing to look at both angles
  >>with vigor and enthusiasm? Can we honestly say this sounds more
  >>like their technology and doesn't resound with aspects of our
  >>own?
  >"Resound with aspects of our own?" You mean the totally silent
  >departure at perhaps supersonic speed? A craft with no wings or
  >other obvious means of support or external propulsion, such as
  >propellers, rotors, jet engines, etc. Lack of all chemical
  >traces at the scene? Fused soil? Bush burned and cut cleanly in
  >half, but with no damage beyond the immediate burn area? What
  >technology did we have back then that could do all this?
  First of all David, congratulations on the diligent patience
  with which your detailed response covered all the
  technical/mechanical issues.
  I hope you won't find it curmudgeonly of me to point out a vital
  factor that your post does not mention. Viktor's explanations
  (if that's what they are - I'm still struggling with the multi-
  angle concept of the jet powered balloon helicopter....) require
  acceptance of something more: poor old Lonnie would have to been
  deceitful or credulous or a half-wit or delusional - or some
  combination of the four. His admitted myopia just isn't enough
  of a causal factor to enable Viktor's explanations to carry any
  weight. Many people met, interrogated and got to know Lonnie in
  the wake of his sighting, but, to the best of my knowledge, it
  was never possible for any of them to impugn his character as a
  credible basis for arriving at an 'explanation'. Just an
  ordinary, decent man who had an extraordinary experience.
  --
  Gerald O'Connell
